### Page 2

2  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/
上週重要市場資訊
 澳洲 8 月就業人數勁增 3.95 萬人大幅優於預期，但勞動參與率上升至 67.1%，
推升失業率至 4.6%，呈現勞動力擴增但市場邊際降溫跡象。澳洲央行總裁布拉
克日前指出，失業率回升至5%有助於抑制頑固通膨。考量物價長期高於目標中
點，市場預期澳洲央行本週二將重啟緊縮，升息 1 碼至 4.60%，創 2011年底以
來新高。
 瑞士央行（SNB）上週四宣布連續第五次將關鍵政策利率維持在0%不變，符合
市場預期。儘管受油價推升使 8 月通膨微升至 0.8%，但仍處於目標區間，央行
預期中期通膨壓力溫和，現行極度寬鬆立場依然適當。決議公布後瑞郎兌美元跌
至 1.2077。相較全球主要央行相繼緊縮，瑞士央行維持超低利率，策略轉為視情
況調節匯市以穩定瑞郎。
 標普全球公布歐元區 9 月綜合 PMI 初值升至 53.1，創三年新高，製造與服務業
同步擴張，新訂單增速達四年多最快，印證經濟無懼美伊衝突引發的能源成本飆

升，展現超預期韌性。受能源價格傳導加速影響，通膨壓力再度升溫，儘管尚未
浮現薪資二輪效應，但強勁景氣數據促使市場大幅提高歐洲央行提前於10月例
會再度升息的押注。
 美國 9 月綜合 PMI 初值勁升至 58.4，創 62 個月新高；製造業與服務業 PMI 亦
逆勢走強至 57與 58.7，凸顯企業擴張強勁，引發市場對通膨持續性的擔憂。市
場焦點轉向 9 月 30日公布的美國 8 月 PCE、個人消費支出與 Q2 GDP終值，同
日日本亦將發布短觀調查與央行意見摘要。若終端消費延續強勁，將牽動美日央
行政策利率預期。

### Page 3

3  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
 美元兌新台幣匯率走勢  
Sep/29/2026

＜上週走勢＞ 
上週台幣匯價高點在 31.655，低點在 31.866，以 31.790作收，較前一周收盤價
升值 2.8分。 
上週台幣先升後貶，周初，台股創新高，外資連續五日買超台股並匯入，推升
台幣最高來到 31.655，不過逢低吸引進口商、政府基金等買盤進場，台幣主要在
31.700 附近震盪；臨近周末，美國 9 月綜合 PMI 由 8 月的 56 走升至 58.4，創下逾
5 年來的新高，加上美伊戰事持續，皆推升市場對後續升息的預期，美元指數升至
101 上方，台幣跳空開低，回貶至 31.800 上方，最低來到 31.866，不過隨後出口商
賣壓出籠，台幣跌勢放緩，最終在央行調節之下收在 31.790。 
＜未來分析＞ 
     預估本週台幣區間震盪。就國際市場而言，美國經濟表現仍具韌性，9月綜合
PMI 由 8 月的 56 上升至 58.4，企業活動加速至逾 5 年來最強，數據公布後美國 10
年債券殖利率升破 5%，Fed Watch 顯示市場對於聯準會 10 月升息預期升高，有助
於美元指數維持偏強格局，預計將給台幣帶來壓力；就台灣市場來看，台股在創下
新高後回落，而外資也結束連續五日買超的走勢，轉為賣超，短線上資金匯入不利
台幣表現，然而，台灣8 月外銷訂單達 1,029.6億美元、年增 71.4%，創歷史新高，
AI 需求帶動出口持續強勁，出口商後續拋匯需求預計將提供台幣支撐。綜合以上，
預估本週台幣區間震盪。 
＜預估本週＞ 美元兌台幣：31.600-32.000

### Page 4

4  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
美元兌人民幣匯率走勢  
Sep/29/2026 
＜上週走勢＞ 
上週人民幣震盪走貶。人民幣中間價在上周連續十天走升，並升破6.7500整數
關口，最高來到 6.7468，帶動人民幣偏升，不過由於美伊戰事持續，市場對於聯準
會升息預期提升，美元指數震盪走強，加上週間公布美國9月綜合 PMI由 8 月的 56
走升至 58.4，創下逾 5 年來的新高，美元指數突破 101 整數關卡，令境外人民幣承
壓，USD/CNH回貶至 6.7200上方，終場境外人民幣收在6.7227，週線貶值283點，
境內人民幣則收在 6.7125，週線貶值 141 點。

＜未來分析＞ 
     預估本週人民幣高檔震盪。就國際市場來看，美中兩國完成新一輪談判，美方
表示雙方同意將原訂 11 月 10 日到期的貿易休戰延長至明年 1 月 10 日，並持續就
關稅、貿易及供應鏈等議題進行協商，降低短期貿易摩擦升溫的風險，對人民幣形
成一定支撐。不過，美國近期經濟數據仍具韌性，加以聯準會偏鷹立場推升美元，
預計將限制人民幣升值空間；就中國國內市場來看，近期人行持續偏強設定中間價，
帶動人民幣維持偏強走勢，惟美中利差再度擴大，預計將限制人民幣升值的空間；
最後就技術面來看，USD/CNH日線圖中，匯價反彈至 20日均線之上，顯示原先下
行動能暫歇，上方壓力先看向季線 6.7350附近，綜合以上，預估本週人民幣高檔震
盪。

＜預估本週＞ 美元兌人民幣：6.6900-6.7400

### Page 5

5  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
美元兌日圓匯率走勢  
         Sep/29/2026

＜上週走勢＞ 
上週 USD/JPY先上後下。週初，美國多位聯準會官員鷹派談話，帶動美元指數
持續上行，推升 USD/JPY延續前一週的走勢來到 157上方；週間，受美國 PMI 強
於預期與美債殖利率全面上行帶動，市場重新提高對聯準會後續升息路徑的押注，
帶動 USD/JPY大漲突破 159的關卡；臨近週末，日本首相高市早苗表態日圓被低估
是個問題，使市場提高對政策訊號與潛在干預的敏感度，帶動USD/JPY回吐大部分
漲幅，終場收在 157.26，週線上漲 56點。 
＜未來分析＞ 
  預估本週 USD/JPY區間震盪，主要原因如下：一、日本財務大臣片山皋月上週
五罕見透露，美國總統川普在美日元首峰會上對日圓過度疲弱表達關切，首相高市
早苗亦回應日圓遭低估確屬問題。儘管近期美元指數維持在 101 上方，但在日本政
府可能干預的陰影下，預計將限縮日圓貶值的空間。二、美國9 月綜合 PMI產出指
數創逾五年新高，疊加美伊戰事推升油價突破100美元與柴油大漲，通膨壓力劇增。
美聯儲理事巴爾表態可能需再升息兩次，芝加哥聯儲古爾斯比也示警能源衝擊具持
續性，推升市場押注 10 月升息機率升至六成，預計將支撐 USD/JPY 走勢。三、就
技術面而言，日線圖中，USD/JPY連日上漲一度站回年線但隨即失守，目前依舊承
壓於季線及年線下方，不過各天期均線趨平，且下方尚有月線支撐，短線尚無明確
的方向。綜合以上，預估 USD/JPY本週呈現區間震盪的可能性較高。 
＜預估本週＞ 美元兌日幣：155.30-159.30 
前週交易區間 
156.56 159.03

### Page 6

6  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
美元兌歐元匯率走勢  
             Sep/29/2026

＜上週走勢＞ 
  上週 EUR/USD 下跌。週初，歐元延續前一週偏弱的走勢，加上聯準會官員鷹
派發言，導致歐元跌破 1.1450的重要關卡；週間，儘管油價自高檔回落，但市場解
讀為能源成本仍處高位、難以迅速回落，推升對美國實質利率與利差優勢的預期。
加上歐洲在能源依賴與成長動能較弱下，相對於美國更難承受長期高利率環境，歐
元最低來到 1.1358；臨近週末，歐元跌深反彈，終場收在1.1391，週線下跌 91點。 
＜未來分析＞ 
  預估本週 EUR/USD 震盪偏弱，主要原因如下：一、歐洲央行首席經濟學家連
恩示警，第二波能源價格飆升將推升通膨，預計至2027年中期始回落至目標水準。
德國央行月報指出，受中東衝突、低天然氣庫存及明年醫療改革影響，通膨將居高
不下；夏季雖因出口疲弱與乾旱致增長趨緩，但在製造業回暖與財政支出挹注下，
估年底經濟有望重啟復甦，預計將提供歐元一定的支撐。二、美聯儲官員接連釋放
強烈鷹派信號，聖路易聯儲總裁穆薩萊姆直言當前 3.75%-4.00%利率仍偏寬鬆，呼
籲盡早收緊；芝加哥聯儲總裁古爾斯比亦警告，AI投資繁榮引發需求過熱，疊加持
續性供應衝擊，可能迫使美聯儲加速且更積極地升息。目前Fedwatch顯示預期明年
4 月前可能再升息三次，將不利歐元表現。三、技術面而言，在日線圖中，EUR/USD
在各天期均線下方，且接連失守1.15及 1.14整數關卡，空方動能較佔優勢。綜合以
上，預估 EUR/USD本週呈現震盪偏弱的可能性較高。 
＜預估本週＞ 歐元兌美元：1.1300-1.1450 
前週交易區間 
1.1358 1.1495

7  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
                           新台幣利率走勢分析

115.09.21-115.09.24 
銀行隔夜拆款利率 0.804% ~ 0.830%  
30天期短票利率   1.65% ~ 1.69%  
公債－10 年期-A15109     1.95%  
－ 5 年期- A15108 1.84%

一、 台幣貨幣市場部分，本周央行持續每日減發定存單，令市場資金充裕，帶動短
率靠向區間低位。 
週二央行標售 2 年期定存單 250 億元，加權平均得標利率為 1.638%，略低於
市場預期，且較上次標售結果下跌 2.5個基本點。 
週四隔夜拆款利率區間在 0.804%~0.830%， 30 天期短票主要成交在
1.65%~1.69%，利率較上週下跌 1 個基本點。 
二、 本週市場交投冷清，10 年期指標券 A15109 及 5 年期指標券 A15108 均未成
交，週四等殖系統參考報價中價分別為 1.95%、1.84%。 
台股排擠效應、國際債市走勢偏空，加上油價續處高檔，通膨潛在威脅仍存，
12 月升息可能性未能完全排除，市場氣氛續偏觀望，預期台債殖利率將維持
高檔區間盤整 ， 預估5年券區間為 1.84%~1.90%，10年券區間為 1.95%~2.0%。

Document 158: a16z深度报告：AI付费市场，已出现不需要登上大众流量榜的生意

Source PDF: all report pdf/a16z深度报告：AI付费市场，已出现不需要登上大众流量榜的生意.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 6
Status: ok

### Page 1

消费者 AI 市场正上演⼀场流量与付费的双轨博弈。 a16z 最新报告揭⽰：仅 4.5% 美国消费者
订阅主流 AI 产品，前 1% 付费⽤⼾⽉均烧⾦ 903 美元， 29 家⽀出榜前 50 ⼚商根本不在任何流
量榜上。 ChatGPT 三榜称霸， Claude 以⾼价订阅逆袭，个⼈代理悄然争夺下⼀个交易⼊
⼝。
摘要：
消费者 AI 市场正在形成⼀个清晰的双轨结构：流量繁荣与付费集中同时存在，⽽真正的商业价值
藏在后者。
a16z 最新⼀期 Top 100 消费者 AI 应⽤追踪报告显⽰，只有 7 家公司同时进⼊⽹⻚流量、移动⽉活
和消费⽀出三张榜单，有 29 家⽀出排名前 50 的⼚商根本不在任何流量榜上。
付费端呈现出极端的幂律特征。前 1% 的付费⽤⼾贡献了 19.5% 的可观察消费，⾼于底部 50% ⽤
⼾合计的 16.6% ；这批重度⽤⼾平均每⽉在 AI 上花费 903 美元，购买的主要是编程、效率和创作
⼯具。截⾄今年 8 ⽉，美国仅有 4.5% 的消费者拥有 ChatGPT 、 Gemini 或 Claude 中⾄少⼀个活跃

个⼈付费订阅。
这⼀结构正在影响整个⾏业的战略⽅向。订阅制在早期帮助 AI 公司覆盖了模型成本，但也将普及
天花板压低。个⼈代理、⼴告、交易抽佣等新商业模式已陆续浮出⽔⾯，指向同⼀个问题：如
何把 AI 从⼀款付费软件，变成普通⼈⽇常⽣活⾥的低⻔槛服务。
榜单趋于稳定，付费数据⾸次成为独⽴维度
本期报告⾸次引⼊了由 YipitData 基于美国消费者银⾏卡及信⽤卡⾯板观察到的 AI ⽀出数据，与
原有的 Similarweb ⽹⻚访问量和 Sensor Tower 移动⽉活并列，形成三条独⽴的观察维度。报告
同期将主要⾯向 NSFW 场景的产品排除在外 —— 按原有⼝径，这类产品会占据⽹⻚流量榜单超过
20% 的份额。
榜单本⾝的流动性已明显下降。本期新增⾸次上榜产品仅 11 个，是七期追踪以来最少的⼀次。
a16z 分析师 Olivia Moore 将这⼀现象背后的核⼼⽭盾概括为：“ 消费者 AI 使⽤很⼴，但对⼏乎所
有⼈来说都不深。 ” 近⼀半美国消费者称⾃⼰在使⽤ AI ，但只有 25% 达到⽇常使⽤频率。

付费数据和流量数据的错位是本期最值得关注的发现。同时进⼊⽹⻚、移动、⽀出三张榜的公
司只有 7 家： ChatGPT 、 Claude 、 Suno 、 Perplexity 、 Photoroom 、 Canva 、 Notion 。 ChatGPT
是唯⼀在三张榜上均排第⼀的产品。
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⽣活家 搜索

### Page 2

Claude 押注⾼价值订阅，与 ChatGPT 打不同的仗
ChatGPT 的整体领先仍⽆争议。 8 ⽉⽹⻚访问量约为 Gemini 的 2 倍、 Claude 的 6 倍；移动端⽉活
则是 Gemini 的 2.5 倍、 Claude 的 14 倍；美国消费者付费订阅数上， ChatGPT 相对 Claude 或
Gemini 也保持约 3 倍优势。
Claude 的增⻓逻辑却与流量排名呈现出明显落差。在美国消费者订阅数上， Claude 今年早些时
候已超过 Gemini ；即便 Google 将历史付费⽤⼾迁移⾄ AI 计划以推⾼订阅数，两者也仅接近打
平 。 Claude 在 2023 年 9 ⽉ 第 ⼀ 期 ⽹ ⻚ 榜 单 中 尚 未 ⼊ 榜 ， 如 今 流 量 已 超 过 DeepSeek 和
Perplexity 。
更关键的差异体现在订阅结构上。 Claude 最贵的个⼈订阅 Max 起价 100 美元 / ⽉，已有 7.3% 的消
费者付费⽤⼾选择这⼀档位； Google 对应档位占⽐为 1.3% ， ChatGPT 为 1.1% 。 Anthropic 在不

依赖⼴告的前提下，正在更积极地挖掘订阅深度。
不过 Claude 的增⻓并⾮直线。 YipitData 全球桌⾯⾯板显⽰， Claude 在 7 ⽉和 8 ⽉的⽇均会话数出
现下滑；美国电⼦收据⾯板中，新增放缓、流失上升。与此同时， ChatGPT 在 7 ⽉推出 GPT-5.6
Sol 、 Terra 、 Luna 及 ChatGPT Work 后重新加速。
三家公司实际上在打三场不同的仗。过去六个⽉， Anthropic 、 Google 、 OpenAI 合计发布 127
次新品： Anthropic ⼏乎全⼒押注专业消费者，如 Claude Design 、 Code Review 、 Claude
Science ； Google 偏向创意模型，如 Lyria 3 Pro 、 Gemini Omni ； OpenAI 则同时覆盖企业和
消费者，从 ChatGPT Work 到图像、健康、个⼈⾦融、求职⼯具全线铺开。

### Page 3

前 1% ⽤⼾撑起付费基础，多订阅⾏为罕⻅
付费基础在扩⼤，但集中度并未稀释。 YipitData 美国电⼦收据⾯板中，今年 8 ⽉有 4.5% 的合格消
费者订阅了⾄少⼀个主流 AI 产品，⼀年前这⼀⽐例为 2.1% 。
在已付费⽤⼾中，多产品订阅⾏为同样罕⻅。为⼀个 AI 产品付费的⼈中，只有 13% 会同时为另
⼀款 AI ⼯具付费。多数⽤⼾的决策逻辑并⾮构建 AI ⼯具矩阵，⽽是找到⼀个能解决明确问题的
产品。
前 1% 付费⽤⼾的画像接近专业消费者：他们⾼频购买⾃动化与产品构建⼯具，如 n8n 、 fal 、
Manus 、 Nous Research 的 Hermes Agent ，同时明显偏好创作⼯具，如 Higgsfield 、 Figma 、
HeyGen 。这批⽤⼾平均每⽉在 AI 上花费 903 美元，购买的本质是⽣产⼒，⽽⾮娱乐。
这⼀结构直接解释了流量榜与收⼊榜的错位。⽀出排名前 50 的⼚商中，有 29 家根本不出现在任
何流量榜上，说明在特定专业⼈群中建⽴深度使⽤，已经⾜以⽀撑可观的商业规模 —— ⽆需依

赖⼤众流量。
个⼈代理争夺交易⼊⼝，平台⽅已开始站队
个⼈代理被视为最接近新商业⼊⼝的⽅向。六个⽉前，此类产品对多数消费者⽽⾔还不够成
熟；⽬前， Instinct 、 Tomo 、 Poke 、 Lindy 、 Town 等创业公司已声称拥有数⼗万⽤⼾。 Meta
推出 Muse ， OpenAI 推出 Dots ， xAI 推出 Grok Bot ，⼤公司也已跟进。
iMessage 成为当前的热⻔渠道。 Instinct 在 2026 年 8 ⽉从这⾥起量， DoorDash 也推出了⾃⼰的
iMessage 点餐代理。这些产品共同争夺的⼊⼝是：像真⼈助理⼀样，可以发短信、发邮件、打
电话。
早期⽤⼾画像仍然偏向硬核。 AssistantBenchmark 创始⼈ David Pawlan 追踪 7 个早期⽤⼾群聊、
共 1502 个样本后发现，最常被讨论的任务是编程和技术⾃动化。代理⽬前还处于替技术⽤⼾处
理⼯作流的阶段，尚未真正进⼊普通消费者的⽇常⽣活。

### Page 4

Instinct 提供了⼀组激进数据：创始⼈ Noah Shinn 称， 40% 的⽤⼾会在三周内绑定个⼈信⽤卡；
⼀旦发⽣购买，这些⽤⼾平均每⽉通过 Instinct 花费 1300 美元；公司年化交易额据称已超过 10 亿
美元，其中⼀半来⾃旅⾏。
平台⽅的态度已分化。 Amazon 在不到两周内切断了 Meta Muse 的访问；⽽ Shopify 、 Instacart 、
OpenTable 、 Expedia 、 Ticketmaster 、 Plaid 等则签下官⽅集成。 Meta Muse 于 9 ⽉ 9 ⽇上线，⾸
周⽇活据称达到 25 万，不到⼀个⽉下载量超过 500 万 —— 但这⼀规模仍远低于 Meta Threads 当
年的启动速度，后者在相同两个市场的前 22 天下载量超过 1500 万。
⼤⼚扩张反⽽圈出创业公司的⽣存空间
成熟产品已⼤规模进场。 Canva 和 Notion 进⼊⽹⻚榜前⼗， Figma 进⼊前⼗五； Superhuman
（原 Grammarly ）⾸次在⽀出榜排⾄第四。 Google ⼀家在⽹⻚端就有五个⼊⼝： Gemini 、

NotebookLM 、 AI Studio 、 Labs 、 Antigravity 。
⼤⼚的全⾯铺开反⽽使创业公司的差异化路径更加清晰。
差异化模型是第⼀个可⾏⽅向。Suno ⽹⻚流量排第 19 、⽀出排第 7 ； ElevenLabs ⽹⻚排第 25 、
⽀出排第 10 。 Midjourney 、 HeyGen 、 Kling 、 Topaz Labs 也进⼊⽀出榜。在创作类任务中，独
特的声⾳、视觉⻛格和训练数据，有时⽐通⽤⼤模型能⼒更具竞争优势。

### Page 5

⻛险提⽰及免责条款
市场有⻛险，投资需谨慎。本⽂不构成个⼈投资建议，也未考虑到个别⽤⼾特殊的投资⽬标、财务状况或需要。⽤⼾应考虑本⽂
中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。

写评论
多模型体验构成第⼆条路径。Cursor ⽀持多家模型， OpenRouter 让开发者接⼊不同供应商。⽤
⼾的核⼼需求并⾮绑定某家模型，⽽是在特定任务中找到最合适的能⼒。 Lovable 、 Cursor 、
Base44 、 Replit 均已进⼊⽹⻚榜， Cursor 从第 41 升⾄第 35 ， Base44 ⾸次上榜排第 46 。
明确⼈群定位是第三个⽅向。OpenEvidence 服务医⽣，进⼊⽹⻚榜第 47 ； Venice 主打私密 AI ，
⾸次上榜排第 43 。特定⼯作流、隐私保护、内容可信度，可能与底层模型能⼒同等重要。内容
政策调整同样留下市场空位 ——NSFW 类产品被主流榜单排除，并不代表需求消失。
重做产品体验是第四条出路，⽬标是让⽤⼾告别空⽩提⽰框。Plaud 将硬件设备与订阅结合，⾸

次进⼊消费者⽀出榜第 16 位。 Meta 将 Muse 延伸⾄智能眼镜和 AI 电⼦宠物， OpenAI 收购 io
Products 则暗⽰了更⼤的硬件布局意图。
订阅撑住早期，⼴告与交易费指向更⼤市场
头部消费者 AI 产品⼏乎全员收费。⽹⻚榜中 44 个 AI 原⽣产品均已实现某种商业化： 84% 提供付
费订阅， 64% 采⽤⽤量收费或额外积分，只有 14% 有⼴告， 2% 通过交易或平台费⽤赚钱。
这与上⼀代消费互联⽹的盈利结构截然相反。⼴告贡献 Meta 收⼊的 97.6% 、 Alphabet 收⼊的
73.2% ；全球前 20 ⼤消费订阅产品中， 65% 属于媒体业务。 AI 公司选择订阅的现实原因是模型成
本⾼企，早期⽆法完全依靠规模换收⼊。
但其他模式已经开始出现。 OpenAI 表⽰， ChatGPT ⼴告在 8 ⽉达到 10 亿美元年化收⼊ run rate ，
基础是 12 亿周活⽤⼾。 OpenEvidence 估计覆盖 50% ⾄ 60% 的美国医⽣，也开始部分依靠⼴告变

现。
交易抽佣被视为代理模式成⽴后的⾃然收费路径：⽤⼾通过 AI 完成可归因购买后，按联盟佣⾦
或抽佣结算。 Instinct 和 Muse 均描述了未来从交易中收费的计划，但⽬前均未实施； ChatGPT
内发起的购买⽬前同样不收费。
消费者 AI 的下⼀个关键问题，已经从 " 是否有⼈愿意为 AI 付钱 " 转变为 " 如何让不愿订阅软件的⼈
开始⾼频使⽤ " 。订阅将持续服务专业⽤⼾群体，⽽⼴告、交易费和代理⼊⼝，才更可能将 AI 带
⼊更⼴泛的⽇常⽣活。

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
The Cotality home value index, covering all of Australia,
fell 1.1% in September with downward revisions to the
previous two months again shaving a further 0.4% off prices.
The correction has now seen prices decline 5.2% from their

March peak. Annual growth is flat.
The price correction remains broad, Cotality reporting 97% of
capital city suburbs experienced falls over the three months
to September, Darwin the only city to record gains. Sydney
and Melbourne continue to see more pronounced declines,
albeit moderating a touch, particularly in Melbourne.
Against this, declines in Brisbane, Adelaide and Perth have
accelerated over the last few months. Across segments, unit
prices and lower price tiers continue to track slightly milder

falls than houses and top tiers.
Seasonal variations accentuated price weakness through winter
but are now neutral. The peak-to-trough price decline nationally
is slightly milder at 4.6% in seasonally adjusted terms. Unless
specified, figures in titles, tables and the commentary below are
in non-seasonally adjusted terms, consistent with the ‘headline’
figures reported by Cotality. However, figures shown in charts
are in seasonally adjusted terms.

Preliminary estimates of turnover show rolling 3mth volumes
down 3.9%qtr in September and 17.4%yr with previous
estimates revised 1-2ppts lower. The monthly pattern
suggests small gains in July and August have given way to
another sizeable fall in September.
Looking at the price performance across different jurisdictions:
• Sydney prices dropped 1.4% fall in September, monthly
declines averaging 1.6% over the previous four months,
tracking a 17% annualised pace of decline since May. All

segments and tiers recorded falls in Q3 but with top and
middle tier houses notably weaker.
• Melbourne prices fell 0.7%mth, a slower pace than other
capitals. This likely reflects the weaker starting point coming
into the correction, prices already back to the levels seen in
mid-2021. As elsewhere, 'top tier' houses prices are leading
the decline. 'Low tier' unit prices mostly held their ground in
Q3, prices in this sub-segment already plumbing 10yr lows.

• Brisbane was the weakest capital city price-wise, recording
a 1.5% drop in September with previous monthly declines
also revised down heavily (August from 1% to 1.6% and
July from 1.2% to 1.7%). The combined effect has taken
the rolling 3mth change to –4.7%qtr from –2.7%qtr last
month and –0.6%qtr in July. At 5.9%yr, annual growth is
still positive but losing altitude quickly, having run at 18%yr
as recently as May. Sellers have been slow to respond to

the sudden stop with total listings up over 50% vs a year
ago, albeit still not high compared to long run averages
(reflecting the extremely tight starting point for the Brisbane
market).
• Adelaide dwelling prices fell 1.3%mth, the August decline

also marked down from 0.8% to 1.1%, annual price growth
dropping to 6.5%yr. The price correction began later in
Adelaide but is now running at a similar pace to prices
nationally.
• Perth recorded a 1.2% decline but also saw big downward
revisions to previous months (August from 0.8% to –1.7%
and July from –1.3% to –1.8%). At 10.1%yr, annual growth
is still solidly positive but was tracking at over 25%yr earlier
in the year. As with Adelaide and Brisbane, the detail shows

a more even spread of declines across price tiers. However,
turnover is showing a milder pull-back in Adelaide.
• The smaller capitals were again more mixed, price growth
still positive in Darwin (0.4%mth, 11.9%yr), relatively
resilient in Hobart (–0.5%mth, 7%yr) but weaker in
Canberra (–1.1%mth, –1.6%yr).
• Regional markets showed a 0.9% decline in Qld but slightly
milder declines in NSW (–0.7%) and Victoria (0.3%). Annual
prices growth is in the 3.4-6.3%yr range.

Our recently released Housing Pulse report (here) provides
a deeper diver into the situation in Australia's housing
markets. While the absence of selling pressure, still tight
supply-demand balance and sentiment mix suggests the
correction was still relatively 'orderly' through the September
quarter, the latest monthly data shows momentum remains
unambiguously negative.
That said, there are clearly more headwinds to come. The
RBA latest interest rate rise and what now looks to be a

more likely prospect of a follow-up increase at its meeting in
early November will impact in coming months. Next week's
consumer sentiment update will show how the change, and
the latest round of weak updates, is shaping housing-related
sentiment. Either way, momentum looks set to remain weak
into year-end and, barring a significantly improved inflation
and interest rate outlook, early 2027.

Document 180: Australia’s Trade in Goods Account, August (2026-10-08)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Australia’s Trade in Goods Account, August (2026-10-08).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-10-08
Pages: 13
Status: ok

### Page 1

#trade #australia
Australia’s Trade in Goods Account, August
By Ryan Wells
Economist, Westpac
13:19 October 01 2026 Share
Another surge in data centre-related equipment imports in August, alongside continued
volatility in gold trade. Trade balance: $0.5bn surplus. Export values: 3.7%mth. Import
values: 5.8%mth.

### Page 2

Australia’s goods trade surplus narrowed to $0.5bn in August from $1.4bn in July (revised down from
$1.9bn previously). This result was significantly lower than expected – the market consensus was for a
broadly stable reading, while Westpac had a slight widening pencilled in. Over the past six months or
so, the trade balance has been oscillating between small deficits and surpluses, largely coinciding with
significant month-to-month volatility in the trade mix. The chief culprit behind this volatility has been

trade in non-monetary gold, among other components.
Before discussing the detail, it is once again worth noting the results for gold up front. In August, non-
monetary gold exports jumped 20%, while imports surged 91%. Excluding gold, the trade figures have
been much more stable, closer to a deficit of $2-3bn per month over the past three months, down from
larger deficits around the start of the year.
Export earnings rose 3.7% in August, bouncing back from a 3.6% decline in July. The trend for exports

so far this year looks a bit more positive, but again, this is tricky to read given the volatility present in
the data. Excluding gold, export earnings were up 1.6% in August. This was largely supported by a
4.7% lift in coal and a 2.8% increase in LNG, with further detail provided by the ABS suggests that an
increase in both volumes and prices contributed. However, iron ore (–1.5%) and rural goods (–1.0%)
weighed on earnings in the month.

### Page 3

The import bill meanwhile increase by 5.8% in August. Once again, a surge in automatic data
processing (ADP) equipment imports – which capture the investment upswing related to data centres –
were in the spotlight, surging 79% in the month up to $4.3bn. There were also an increase in other
capital goods imports centred on telecommunications equipment (+12%) and civil aircraft (+30%) –
large in their own right but dwarfed by the surge in ADP imports in the month.

Our latest estimates have the total pipeline for data centres at around $175bn over the coming decade,
with much of that being front-loaded. It is natural to expect month-to-month volatility in ADP equipment
imports as these large and lumpy data centre projects are worked through, but it is worth highlighting
that August’s jump closely resembles the surge from March, when imports also surged to around
$4.3bn. Given the scale of the increase in August, Q3 is shaping up to be another quarter where data

centre investment will play a significant role in driving growth – while it is true that the data centre build-
out is import-intensive, our modelling suggests that once spending enters Australia, the local economic
impact is likely to be around half of the total investment.

### Page 4

Elsewhere across the import basket, while gold imports also surged, it was effectively offset by a
decline in consumer imports of non-industrial transport equipment (i.e. cars), down 21.2% in August
following the surge from May to July. While still tentative at this stage, it suggests that the momentum in
EV purchases, which played a significant role in the consumer spending story in Q2, may be starting to
wane. Additionally, a further pull-back in fuel imports (–5.0%) in August has extended the downward

trend in recent months, following the initial surge in oil prices in March and April after the war in the
Middle East began. However, with the conflict re-escalating recently and margins on refined fuel
products widening, we could see this trend reverse over the coming months.

Document 188: Capital Market Outlook (2026-09-28)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Capital Market Outlook (2026-09-28).pdf
Report source: Merrill Lynch (CMO)
Date: 2026-09-28
Pages: 8
Status: ok

### Page 1

CHIEF INVESTMENT OFFICE
Capital Market Outlook

### Page 2

2 of 8 September 28, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
The Five Shocks of 2026—And the Extraordinary Resilience of the U.S. Economy
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
Lauren Sanfilippo, Director and Senior Investment Strategist
This is the year the consensus got mugged.
Rewind to the start of the year. The market consensus was that the U.S. economy was
set to slow. The Fed was set to cut interest rates. Corporate earnings were set to grow—

but not explode. Energy prices would remain tame. And the odds looked good that the
Republicans would control the House, the Senate, or both, following the midterm election.
Nine months on, the landscape looks starkly different. Defined by five shocks—energy,
interest rates, earnings, growth, and politics—2026 looks nothing like the year investors
thought they were buying on January 1.
Shock 1: Diesel Matters. While global oil prices have moved higher since the start of

the U.S./Iran war on February 28, oil prices have yet to surpass their previous record high
because the world has released some 400 million barrels from strategic reserves this year,
China has curbed its demand for oil imports, and the U.S. has goosed its energy exports to
record highs.
But here is the problem: While gasoline powers the American consumer, diesel powers the
American economy—and diesel prices are now sitting at record highs. Diesel supplies have

been hammered by wars in the Middle East and Ukraine that have sharply reduced refining
capacity. According to the International Energy Agency, by August, exports of diesel from
refiners in the Middle East had fallen by a quarter from prewar levels. Meanwhile, Ukraine
has hit 24 of Russia’s 34 largest refineries, reducing supplies from one of the world’s
largest diesel exporters. The upshot: U.S. diesel prices have soared from $3.55 a gallon
at the start of the year to roughly $6.52 a gallon. While crude oil trades for around $100

a barrel, the retail equivalent of a barrel of diesel now costs more than $260. The diesel
shock of 2026 was the last thing investors saw coming—and remains a key impediment to
structurally lower inflation.
Shock 2: From Interest Rate Cuts to Rate Hikes. Perhaps no other consensus view
entering 2026 has been turned more dramatically on its head than expectations for the
Fed. Heading into 2026, markets were pricing in rate cuts on expectations of a cooling

labor market, moderating inflation, and slower economic growth. The Fed easing cycle was
expected to continue in 2026. In January 2026, futures showed a roughly 50% chance of a
cut by March.
Expectations, however, flipped in late February with the start of the U.S./Iran war. The
Middle East energy shock reignited inflationary pressures, which, combined with stronger-
than-expected U.S. economic growth and a tightening labor market, have demolished the

odds of easier money in 2026. Short- and long-term Treasury yields rose sharply last week
in the wake of the S&P Global Flash U.S. Composite Purchasing Managers’ Index (PMI)
which pointed to the strongest business expansion in more than five years and the fastest
employment growth in over four years, while also reinforcing expectations that the Fed
will maintain a restrictive policy stance for longer. Markets were expecting two to three
quarter-point rate cuts in 2026; instead, the Fed has already raised rates once this year and

is expected to keep raising rates over the near term.
Shock 3: The Earnings Boom No One Saw Coming. Not all the shocks of 2026 are
negative. Indeed, stronger-than-expected corporate earnings have been among the most
positive factors of this year. Corporate America entered the year facing questions about
tariffs, AI-related capital expenditure spending, the health of the U.S. consumer, operating
leverage and the U.S. dollar outlook. At the start of the year, Wall Street was expecting

roughly $290 of S&P 500 earnings per share for 2026, per Bloomberg estimates. But by
September 21, the estimate had risen by $50, to $340 per share.
Investment Implications
While some of these shocks may
contribute to periods of market
volatility, the combination of strong
earnings growth, robust domestic
demand, and ongoing capital
investment suggests that the
fundamental backdrop for Equities
remains constructive.

### Page 3

3 of 8 September 28, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
Earnings have been upgraded and underpinned by several factors, including robust final 
domestic demand in the U.S., a pickup in global growth, AI-related capital spending, 
productivity gains, and enhanced operating leverage. All these variables have helped deliver 
an earnings “shock” to Wall Street. And as an aside, yes, headline profit growth has been 
inflated by strong technology earnings and one-off investment gains from Amazon and 
Alphabet. That said, the S&P 500’s earnings story remains extraordinary. According to 
FactSet, the 493 companies excluding the Magnificent 7 1 
posted 31.8% earnings growth in 
Q2, the strongest level since late 2021.

Shock 4: An Economy with more Horsepower than the Headlines Suggest. That 
corporate profits have exceeded expectations this ye
ar is due in no small part to an economy 
that continues to surprise to the upside. The Fed was supposed to cut rates this year to 
keep the economy humming and prevent it from losing steam. But rather than limp through 
2026, the economy has leaped ahead. Yes, real gross domestic product (GDP) growth of 
2.1% in Q1 and 1.5% in Q2 are hardly blockbuster annualized rates of growth. However, the 
figures belie the underlying strength of the U.S. economy, with real final domestic demand

(which strips out trade) running at a 4.2% annual rate in Q2. According to the latest reading 
of the Atlanta Fed GDP tracker, Q3 real GDP grow
th is running at a red-hot rate of 5.1%. 
Slowdown? What slowdown?
Resilient consumer spending, robust capital investment, record leve
ls of new business 
formation, and strong foreign direct investment inflows have combined to stoke growth 
in the $32 trillion behemoth called the U.S. economy—the most resilient and dynamic 
economy in the world.
Shock 5: The Unexpected Washington Reset. The last shock is still in the making, but 
given current trends, the odds are rising that the Democrats could control both the House 
and Senate follow
ing the midterm elections. To this point, in January, and per Kalshi, the 
Democrats had a 71% shot at the House and an 18% chance of taking the Senate. Fast-
forward to today, and the odds are a 90% chance that the Democrats take the House and a 
60% chance that they take the Senate.
How all of this plays out remains to be seen—there are some 36 days before the November 
vote, and anything can happen. The market base case remains that the Democrats take the 
House, and the Senate is a toss-up—or too close to call. But with the Democrats expected to 
control at least one chamber of Congress, inv
estors can expect more volatility and uncertainty 
about negotiations ov
er the budget, debt ceiling, defense spending and AI regulation. Another 
political wildcard to watch: the changing dynamics within the Democratic Party.
The Biggest Shock of All for 2026? Pe
rhaps it’s not $6 diesel, rising interest rates, 
solid growth, booming profits or shifting politics. Rather, it’s the ability of the U.S. 
economy to absorb the unexpected, not buckle under stress, and continue generating  wealth 
for investors. To wit, the S&P 500 is on pace for its fourth
 consecutive year of double-digit 
returns.

4 of 8 September 28, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MARKET VIEW
2026 Has, So Far, Defied the Traditional Midterm Year Playbook
Jordy Fuentes, Assistant Vice President and Investment Strategist
Current Landscape. To no surprise, market volatility persisted in 2026. Performance initially
tracked historical midterm election patterns before diverging meaningfully as the year
progressed, though there are still three months to go. Several items remain at hand as U.S.

voters head to the polls in November, including geopolitical and foreign policy uncertainty,
energy driven inflation pressures, affordability concerns, U.S. fiscal trajectory, and rapid
advancements in AI. Regardless of the eventual political outcome, history suggests that
markets and the economy can perform well across different configurations of government.
In our view, markets continue to be anchored by strong fundamentals and encouraging
seasonal trends.

To Be Addressed. U.S. domestic and foreign policy remain in focus as the global reorder,
tit-for-tat tariffs, and geopolitical conflicts spanning the Middle East to Eastern Europe have
added layers of uncertainty to the U.S. economic outlook.
At the same time, persistent geopolitical flare-ups have pushed oil prices materially
higher, adding to inflation pressures and keeping them above the Fed’s 2% target.
Headline Consumer Price Index (CPI) held above 3% year-over-year (YoY) in August, while

its core measure, which excludes food and energy, held at 2.4% YoY. The latest Personal
Consumption Expenditure (PCE), the Fed’s preferred measure of inflation, also remained
above 3% YoY for both its headline and core figures. 2
The U.S. fiscal trajectory is another area of focus. The U.S. budget deficit stands at 5.4% of
GDP , just above this century’s average of 4.6%. Meanwhile, U.S. public sector debt surpassed
the $40 trillion mark for the first time in August, oscillating around 100% of GDP .3 While these

trends warrant close monitoring, we view them as a slow-moving structural headwind rather
than an immediate concern, particularly within a highly innovative and diverse U.S. economy.
AI has emerged as a policy consideration, with debates increasingly centered on both
regulatory safeguards and the economic implications of the expanding infrastructure buildout.
For voters, those concerns extend to affordability, as the significant energy requirements of

new data centers raise questions about potential pressure on electricity costs.
Strap In for The Ride. Leading up to the midterm election, markets tend to experience
greater swings as investors navigate uncertainty surrounding the policy path for the
remainder of the presidential cycle (Exhibit 2A). Since 1930, the S&P 500 has averaged an
intrayear drawdown of 20% in midterm years, with the latest election in 2022 experiencing
a 25% intrayear pullback. This year, however, the S&P 500 largest peak-to-trough drawdown

has been 9%, considerably shallower than its long-term average (Exhibit 2B). Yet while
volatility may rise as election day approaches, 2026 has increasingly defied the traditional
midterm year playbook.
Why 2026 is Different. Despite entering a challenging stretch of the presidential cycle,
U.S. Equities have materially outpaced the typical year two pattern (Exhibit 2C). The S&P
500 experienced its best Q2 in a midterm year since 1938, surging 14.9% compared to an

average decline of 1.6%. From a monthly perspective, September stands out as the weakest
month, though month-to-date performance points to an unusual 1.0% rise. That resilience
follows a 2.6% gain in August, another month in which performance meaningfully exceeded
the historical decline of 0.5% (Exhibit 2D).
Through September, U.S. Equity resilience has also extended beyond headline returns with
both the frequency and magnitude of market pullbacks remaining relatively subdued. Market

corrections of 10% or more have occurred in just 29% of midterm election years since 1930.4
Against this backdrop, the market’s last correction came in spring 2025, while this year has
2 Bloomberg, Bureau of Labor Statistics. Data through August 31, 2026.

weakness could represent a

5 of 8 September 28, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
seen only one 5%+ pullback compared to the average three per year, underscoring relatively
resilient market conditions.5 Year-to-date, the S&P 500 has increased 13%, well above the
average annual return of 3% according to Bloomberg through September 25, and on pace
to mark the strongest midterm election year since 2006. Taken together, 2026 has thus far
defied the traditionally weak midterm year seasonal pattern, reinforcing that every election

cycle is unique and historical trends may be a useful guide to navigate market volatility.
What’s Ahead? Forward performance is increasingly constructive for Equities. In the
post-war period, the S&P 500 rose 100% of the time in the six and 12 months following
elections, averaging 13% and 14%, respectively. 6 Even narrowing the time frame to three
months, markets ended higher 90% of the time. As investors push through the finish line,
the last three months of the year tend to be the strongest with October standing out,

ending higher 67% of the time. On average, Q4 has gained 5.6% since 1930, far surpassing
the preceding three quarters of election years. 7 Greater clarity about the U.S. fiscal policy
path serves as a potential tailwind for investors following the election.
To Take Home. Volatility tends to rise during midterm years, but history suggests that
reacting to short-term political uncertainty has rarely rewarded long-term investors. Over
longer horizons, the data suggests Equity returns and economic strength are shaped less

by election outcomes than by corporate profits, valuations, labor market conditions and
capital investments.

THE TICKER TAPE
What is the bond market telling us?
Christopher Hyzy, Chief Investment Office
We believe the bond market is telling us that the Fed is on track to meeting their dual
mandate of price stability and full employment. More importantly, when examining the
full yield curve the 2-year Treasury yield is currently around 4.88%, which typically is
the most sensitive to the fed funds rate and tends to price in future potential policy

adjustments. At the same time, the 10-year Treasury yield is currently about 5.11%. This
leads us to believe that the market expects the Fed to engineer a couple of more hikes
while the back end of the curve is indicating that economic growth is still healthy.
Taken together, it appears the full curve, which has clearly flattened, believes the Fed is
doing its job, while supply shocks, for obvious reasons, are not leading to higher long-run
inflation. The backup in long-dated yields is growth, not necessarily inflation as indicated

by rising “real” yields. Therefore, we view the drift higher in yields as a more normal sign
than what many have originally suggested. We continue to emphasize higher economic
growth for longer, rising productivity in the medium term, supply shortages to remain in
key areas within the AI space, and shocks in the energy equation. We expect the energy
shocks to ultimately fade away over time, but we do not believe that growth fades
anytime soon. In our view, a more normal bond market pricing in Fed moves at the short

end, and growth at the long end helps financial conditions and economic growth operate
in a more durable manner.
In summary, the front-end of the curve expects 3 to 4 more hikes in the next 18 months
or so. This can be debated obviously based on future data especially short-term energy
supply shocks. Long-end yields are drifting higher based on five main factors below, in
our view.
1. Strong economic growth (both
services and manufacturing)
2. Hawkish Fed

3. Debt and deficit concerns
4. Lower demand for long duration
sovereign debt in general
5. Energy supply shocks
The largest weight being strong economic activity which kick starts portfolio
repositioning particularly as the Fed hikes rates. The repositioning stops once demand
rises again when yields reach attractive levels for the more patient longer term
investment community. The supply shocks ultimately end and debt and deficit worries will
likely remain, but they still take a backseat for now. Longer duration government debt is

becoming more attractive each week even in the face of the concerns above. Be patient

Index Definitions
Securities indexes assume reinvestment of all distributions and interest payments. Indexes are unmanaged and do not take into account fees or expenses. It is not possible to
invest directly in an index. Indexes are all based in U.S. dollars.
S&P 500 Index is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States.

S&P 500 Index Price Return is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States. A price return index is a stock
market index that tracks only the price changes and capital gains or losses of its component securities, without including dividends or cash distributions.
S&P Global Flash U.S. Composite Purchasing Managers’ Index is an early monthly economic indicator that estimates overall private-sector business activity by combining manufacturing

Investing involves risk, including the possible loss of principal. Past performance is no guarantee of future results.

or sale of any security or investment strategy. Merrill offers a broad range of brokerage, investment advisory and other services. There are important differences between brokerage and
investment advisory services, including the type of advice and assistance provided, the fees charged, and the rights and obligations of the parties. It is important to understand the differences,
particularly when determining which service or services to select. For more information about these services and their differences, speak with your Merrill financial advisor.

oversight and data analytics. CIO viewpoints are developed for Bank of America Private Bank, a division of Bank of America, N.A., (“Bank of America”) and Merrill Lynch, Pierce, Fenner & Smith

The Global Wealth & Investment Management Investment Strategy Committee (“GWIM ISC”) is responsible for developing and coordinating recommendations for short-term and long-term
investment strategy and market views encompassing markets, economic indicators, asset classes and other market-related projections affecting GWIM.

of Bank of America Corporation.

of all investors.
Asset allocation, diversification and rebalancing do not ensure a profit or protect against loss in declining markets.
Investments have varying degrees of risk. Some of the risks involved with equity securities include the possibility that the value of the stocks may fluctuate in response to events specific to

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.
Alternative investments are speculative and involve a high degree of risk.
Alternative investments are intended for qualified investors only. Alternative Investments such as derivatives, hedge funds, private credit, private equity funds, and funds of funds can result

in higher return potential but also higher loss potential. Changes in economic conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect your investments. Before you invest in alternative
investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.

VND (+0.5% yoy), trong đó thu nhập tài chính tăng mạnh bù đắp phần lớn sự suy 
giảm lợi nhuận từ hoạt động khai thác nhà ga.

Chúng tôi kỳ vọng mùa cao điểm hàng hóa cuối năm và mạng bay Trung Đông 
phục hồi sẽ hỗ trợ sản lượng SCS trong những tháng cuối năm. Tuy nhiên, mức độ 
cải thiện còn hạn chế do nhu cầu vận tải trên các tuyến Trung Đông – châu Á và 
châu Âu – Trung Đông vẫn suy yếu. Sản lượng hàng quốc tế/nội địa năm 2026 dự 
kiến đạt 209/52 nghìn tấn (-7%/-4% yoy).

Chúng tôi kỳ vọng sau khi các hạ tầng kết nối với sân bay Long Thành được hoàn 
thiện, tỷ lệ phân bổ chuyến bay quốc tế giữa Long Thành và Tân Sơn Nhất sẽ dao 
động quanh khoảng 75%/25%. Tỷ trọng phân bổ hàng quốc tế tại TP. Hồ Chí Minh 
qua Long Thành 2027/2028/2029 dự kiến sẽ đạt 10%/35%/55%.

Chúng tôi kỳ vọng SCS tiếp tục duy trì cổ tức tiền mặt cao nhờ khả năng tạo dòng 
tiền ổn định và quy mô tiền gửi lớn. Cổ tức năm 2026 dự kiến đạt 5,000 VND/cổ 
phiếu, trước khi duy trì ở mức 3,000 VND/cổ phiếu/năm từ 2027 nhằm tích lũy 
nguồn lực cho các dự án mới.

Dựa trên triển vọng kinh doanh và kết quả định giá, chúng tôi đưa ra khuyến nghị 
TRUNG LẬP với cổ phiếu SCS, mức giá mục tiêu 50,700 VND/cổ phiếu.

Cơ cấu doanh thu 2025 Hoạt động kinh doanh

CTCP Dịch vụ hàng hóa Sài Gòn được thành 
lập năm 2008, hiện là đơn vị khai thác nhà ga 
hàng hóa hàng không lớn thứ 2 tại cảng 
hàng không quốc tế (HKQT) Tân Sơn Nhất 
với thị phần khoảng 50%. Các cổ đông lớn 
của SCS bao gồm CTCP Gemadept (33.4%) 
và Tổng Công ty Cảng Hàng không Việt Nam 
(13.6%). 
Hoạt động khai thác nhà ga là mảng kinh 
doanh chính của SCS. Hiện SCS đang tiếp 
tục mua sắm trang thiết bị nâng công suất 
xử lý hàng lên 350,000 tấn/năm. 
Nguồn: Báo cáo công ty, KBSV

Điểm nhấn đầu tư  Chú thích 
Sản lượng hàng quốc tế/nội địa năm 2026 dự kiến đạt 209/52 nghìn tấn (-7%/-4% yoy) dù đã được hỗ 
trợ bởi mạng bay Trung Đông phục hồi và mùa cao điểm cuối năm 
 Chi tiết ở phần dưới của báo cáo

Tân Sơn Nhất phân bổ sang cho L TA vào 2027/2028/2029/2030, trước khi bắt đầu tăng trưởng trở lại từ 
2031. 
 Chi tiết ở phần dưới của báo cáo 
SCS tiếp tục duy trì chính sách cổ tức tiền mặt ổn định nhờ nền tảng tài chính lành mạnh, cổ tức 2026 
dự kiến đạt 5,000 VND/cổ phiếu và duy trì ở mức 3,000 VND/cổ phiếu/năm từ 2027.

KQKD 2Q2026 giảm nhẹ

2Q2026, SCS ghi nhận doanh thu 279 tỷ VND (-4% yoy), LNST đạt 188 tỷ VND (đi 
ngang yoy). Lũy kế 1H2026, doanh thu đạt 551 tỷ VND (-1% yoy), LNST đạt 361 
tỷ VND (+0.5% yoy), trong đó thu nhập tài chính tăng mạnh bù đắp phần lớn sự 
suy giảm lợi nhuận từ hoạt động khai thác nhà ga.

### Page 4

Biểu đồ 4. Cơ cấu thông lượng hàng SCS (tấn)  Biểu đồ 5. Giá dịch vụ và biên LNG hoạt động khai thác nhà ga 
của SCS

Nguồn: SCS  Nguồn: SCS

Mạng bay Trung Đông phục hồi hỗ 
trợ sản lượng SCS trong 2H2026, 
song mức độ cải thiện còn hạn chế 
 Chúng tôi kỳ vọng sản lượng hàng hóa của SCS sẽ cải thiện trong những tháng 
cuối năm 2026 nhờ mạng bay Trung Đông dần được khôi phục, đặc biệt từ 
Qatar Airways, cùng nhu cầu vận tải hàng hóa tăng trong mùa cao điểm cuối 
năm. Tuy nhiên, mức độ phục hồi dự kiến còn hạn chế khi nhu cầu vận tải trên 
các tuyến Trung Đông – Châu Á và Châu Âu – Trung Đông vẫn giảm lần lượt 
14.1% và 16.1% yoy trong tháng 7/2026 (tháng thứ 5 liên tiếp), trái ngược với 
mức tăng 3.9% của thị trường hàng hóa hàng không toàn cầu. Diễn biến này 
phần nào giải thích việc sản lượng hàng quốc tế của SCS tiếp tục giảm khoảng 
10% yoy trong tháng 7–8, dù tần suất bay của các hãng Trung Đông đã phục 
hồi đáng kể.

Chúng tôi dự phóng sản lượng hàng quốc tế và nội địa trong bốn tháng cuối 
năm lần lượt giảm 4% và 2% yoy, cả năm 2026 đạt 209.4 nghìn tấn (-7% yoy) và 
52.2 nghìn tấn (-4% yoy). Doanh thu khai thác nhà ga được kỳ vọng tiếp tục 
được hỗ trợ bởi việc điều chỉnh tăng giá dịch vụ từ đầu năm, bù đắp một phần 
tác động từ sản lượng suy giảm.

Biểu đồ 6. Mức độ phục hồi chuyến bay của các hãng hàng không vùng Vịnh 
Đến ngày 21/09/2026, tần suất bay của các 
hãng vùng Vịnh đã phục hồi đáng kể, phần 
lớn đạt khoảng 85–100% mức trước xung đột, 
riêng Qatar Airways đạt 97%, tăng từ mức 
87% ghi nhận giữa tháng 6.

### Page 5

Long Thành tạo áp lực lên sản 
lượng và biên lợi nhuận từ 2027, 
triển vọng dài hạn phụ thuộc khả 
năng tham gia vận hành nhà ga mới 
 Chúng tôi kỳ vọng nhu cầu vận tải hàng hóa hàng không khu vực phía Nam duy 
trì tăng trưởng trong trung hạn, với sản lượng hàng quốc tế/nội địa tăng 8%/4% 
yoy năm 2027 và 9%/6% mỗi năm từ 2028, được hỗ trợ bởi hoạt động xuất 
nhập khẩu, thương mại điện tử và sự phát triển của chuỗi cung ứng. Tuy nhiên, 
sản lượng hàng quốc tế qua SCS dự kiến chịu áp lực khi các chuyến bay từng 
bước chuyển sang Long Thành. Trong kịch bản cơ sở, chúng tôi giả định tỷ lệ 
chuyến bay quốc tế chuyển sang Long Thành lần lượt đạt 10%/35%/55%/70% 
trong giai đoạn 2027–2030, với tác động lên sản lượng thực tế phụ thuộc vào 
tiến độ khai thác nhà ga hàng hóa và hệ thống logistics tại sân bay mới.

Đồng thời, giá dịch vụ quốc tế dự kiến bắt đầu giảm từ giữa năm 2027 nhằm giữ 
chân các hãng hàng không, trong khi giá nội địa kỳ vọng duy trì tăng 3%/năm. 
Theo đó, biên lợi nhuận gộp dự kiến giảm nhẹ trong năm 2027 và chịu áp lực 
đáng kể từ 2028 khi tỷ trọng hàng quốc tế có mức giá dịch vụ cao suy giảm.

Bên cạnh đó, SCS có cơ hội mở rộng quy mô khai thác trong dài hạn thông qua 
việc tham gia vận hành nhà ga hàng hóa số 1 tại Long Thành, công suất thiết kế 
550 nghìn tấn/năm. Doanh nghiệp đã ký biên bản ghi nhớ với ACV về khả năng 
tham gia vận hành nhà ga này, qua đó có thể tiếp cận nguồn hàng dịch chuyển 
từ Tân Sơn Nhất nếu được lựa chọn. Tuy nhiên, chúng tôi chưa đưa nguồn thu 
từ dự án vào kịch bản cơ sở khi chưa có kết quả lựa chọn đơn vị vận hành chính 
thức.

Biểu đồ 7. Thông lượng hàng và biên LNG của SCS 2022 – 2028F

Nguồn: SCS, KBSV dự phóng

Nền tảng tài chính lành mạnh và 
chính sách cổ tức tiền mặt ổn định 
hỗ trợ định giá cổ phiếu 
 Chúng tôi kỳ vọng SCS tiếp tục duy trì chính sách cổ tức tiền mặt ổn định trong 
những năm tới nhờ khả năng tạo dòng tiền cao, quy mô tiền gửi lớn và mức 
đòn bẩy tài chính thấp. Trong 1H2026, thu nhập tài chính tăng mạnh đã bù đắp 
phần lớn sự suy giảm lợi nhuận từ hoạt động khai thác nhà ga, giúp LNST duy 
trì ổn định ở mức 361 tỷ VND (+0.5% yoy). Chúng tôi dự phóng cổ tức tiền mặt 
năm 2026 tiếp tục đạt 5,000 VND/cổ phiếu, trước khi duy trì ở mức 3,000 
VND/cổ phiếu/năm từ 2027, phù hợp với triển vọng lợi nhuận chịu áp lực từ 
Long Thành và nhu cầu tích lũy nguồn lực tài chính để tham gia các dự án mới.

Biên LNST 63% 66% +3 ppts 65% -1 ppt

Nguồn: Báo cáo công ty, KBSV

Khuyến nghị TRUNG LẬP, giá mục 
tiêu 50,700 VND/cổ phiếu  
 Sử dụng phương pháp chiết khấu dòng tiền (DCF), trên cơ sở cập nhật triển 
vọng kinh doanh và tác động từ quá trình chuyển đổi khai thác sang Long 
Thành, chúng tôi đưa ra giá mục tiêu cho SCS là 50,700 VND/cổ phiếu, tương 
đương với mức tiềm năng sinh lời 14.3% so với mức giá đóng cửa ngày 
22/09/2026. Trong kịch bản cơ sở, chúng tôi chưa đưa nguồn thu từ việc tham 
gia vận hành nhà ga hàng hóa số 1 tại Long Thành vào định giá. Các giả định 
chính bao gồm: 
- Thông lượng hàng quốc tế năm 2026 đạt 209.4 nghìn tấn (-7% yoy), với 
bốn tháng cuối năm giảm 4% yoy do mạng bay Trung Đông chưa phục 
hồi hoàn toàn. Nhu cầu hàng quốc tế khu vực phía nam dự kiến tăng 
8% năm 2027 và 9%/năm trong giai đoạn 2028–2030, kéo theo lượng 
hàng qua SCS trong 2027/2028/2029/2030 sẽ giảm lần lượt -2.8%/-
21%/-25%/-27% yoy, trước khi tăng trưởng trở lại từ 2031 sau khi đạt 
tỷ trọng phân bổ tối ưu giữa SGN và LTA. 
- Thông lượng hàng quốc nội năm 2026 đạt 52.2 nghìn tấn (-4% yoy), với 
bốn tháng cuối năm giảm 2% yoy do áp lực chi phí vận tải và điều 
chỉnh mạng bay. Sản lượng dự kiến phục hồi 4% năm 2027 và 6%/năm 
từ 2028 khi chi phí nhiên liệu hạ nhiệt và năng lực khai thác cải thiện. 
- Giá nội địa dự kiến tăng 3%/năm, trong khi giá quốc tế bắt đầu giảm 
từ giữa 2027 nhằm giữ chân các hãng hàng không. Biên lợi nhuận gộp 
dự kiến giảm nhẹ trong 2027 và chịu áp lực đáng kể từ 2028 khi tỷ 
trọng hàng quốc tế suy giảm và chi phí cố định không giảm tương ứng

Document 206: De-reservification, Not De-dollarization (2026-09-15)

Source PDF: all report pdf/De-reservification, Not De-dollarization (2026-09-15).pdf
Report source: CFR (Follow the Money)
Date: 2026-09-15
Pages: 16
Status: ok

### Page 1

1
EXPERT TAKE FOLLOW THE MONEY
De-reservification, Not De-dollarization
The world isn’t moving away from the dollar. It is shifting its dollars from traditional
reserves to state banks, pension funds, and other quasi-sovereign investors.
(Bernardo Montoya/Courtesy Reuters)
Articles Follow the Money Share
Log In
Regions Topics Research Experts Events The Future of American Strategy

### Page 2

2
PUBLISHED
September 15, 2026 8:37 a.m.
Brad W. Setser CFR EXPERT
Whitney Shepardson Senior Fellow
This is a joint blog with Alex Etra, Senior Macro
Strategist and head of Flow of Funds at Vanda
Research.
The IMF’s data on the currency composition of
foreign exchange (FX) reserves is scrutinized carefully
for any signs that the world is shifting way from the
dollar.
Yet it doesn’t really matter that much if the dollar’s
share of reserves has shifted from 56.5 percent to 57

percent. The ink-to-impact ratio of the quarterly
reporting on the latest COFER data is all off.
Neither the stock of global reserves nor the stock of
dollar reserves has changed much in the last ten years.
The action is elsewhere.

### Page 3

3
China’s state banks (per the Bank of International
Settlements) have almost as many foreign assets as the
central bank (PBOC).
Japan’s Government Pension Investment Fund
(GPIF) has almost as many foreign assets ($986
billion) as the government has FX reserves ($1.1
trillion at the end of August). Japan’s FX reserves are
on the books of the Ministry of Finance and the
GPIF is on the books of the Ministry of Health,
Welfare and Labor—so these are almost all assets of

the Government of Japan, not its central bank.
Korea’s National Pension Service has more foreign
assets than the Bank of Korea has FX reserves.
A different dynamic is in play in Taiwan, as the
government doesn’t run a big retirement fund. But in
a world where the hedge ratio of the life insurers has
been (and still is) used as a policy tool, the shift in

### Page 4

4
Taiwan’s foreign asset accumulation away from the
Central Bank of China (CBC) and over to
institutions whose activities in the foreign currency
market it directly influences broadly fits into the same
theme.
In the last year, the lifers’ hedge ratios—and their
direct hedges with the central bank—have been one
of the key tools the CBC has used to manage the
Taiwan dollar (and guard the lifers’ solvency).
Asia’s big surplus economies are in a sense just

catching up with the world’s oil exporters. Norway
put its oil surplus into a sovereign wealth fund from
the start. Kuwait, Qatar, and Abu Dhabi (the most
oil-rich emirate) all also have large sovereign funds.
And the world’s biggest oil exporter has joined them:
The Public Investment Fund has almost as many
foreign assets as the Saudi Central Bank (SAMA).*
What’s more, the available evidence suggests that the
dollar share of these pools is in line with or higher

than the dollar share of global FX reserves.
In other words, by looking at the (easily available)
data on the world’s static holdings of formal FX
reserve assets, scholars and analysts miss most of the
growth in the world’s sovereign and quasi-sovereign
foreign assets.
Consider China.

### Page 5

5
No serious analyst now disputes that China’s state
banks—including the policy banks (the China
Development Bank, China Exim)—hold several
trillion in foreign assets.
China reports $3.3 trillion in gross foreign assets to
the BIS (the net position, counting foreign bank
claims on the entire Chinese economy not just the
banks, is $2.5 trillion). That maps to the BOP data,
which shows almost $4 trillion in gross outflows
through the banking system (technically, the sum of

gross outflows in “other” plus the $500 billion in
foreign currency bonds held by the state commercial
banks)
Since 2010 (and even more so after 2014) the net
outflow through the state banks has exceeded reserve
accumulation.

### Page 6

6
The broad contours of this story are confirmed by the
balance sheet data reported by state commercial
banks in their 2025 annual reports, which showed
that the top five banks held a combined $2.5 trillion
in foreign currency assets (mostly held abroad).
The Chinese haven’t disclosed the foreign assets of
the two policy banks (with at this stage the complicity

### Page 7

7
of the IMF, which has neither analyzed the role of
SAFE policy bank financing, nor highlighted the
glaring gap in China’s own reporting). But the work
of AidData points to nearly $1 trillion in foreign
assets, with a hefty dollar share.
The available data sources all suggest that the bulk of
the foreign assets of the state banks are in dollars.
SAFE’s disclosure—which covers the commercial
banks—puts the dollar share of their offshore foreign
currency assets at around 70 percent. That is well

above the last disclosed dollar share of China’s formal
reserves (55 percent in 2019). The banks’ annual
reports suggest that about 70 percent of the banks’
assets are in dollars and Hong Kong dollars (55
percent USD/15 percent HKD)—a bit below the
dollar and Hong Kong dollar share of their liabilities
(swaps out of the HKD fund a decent share of the
banks euro assets).
Put simply, SAFE’s static dollar holdings aren’t the
important story.
The real story is the rapid growth of the state banks

dollar holdings.
Japan holds a high (though undisclosed) dollar share
in its FX reserves, and those reserves are primarily
invested in Treasuries—so Japan’s MoF is now clearly
the largest contributor to the U.S. data on foreign
official holdings of Treasuries. (SAFE has shifted its

### Page 8

8
funds out of U.S. custodians, and thus increasingly
appears in the data as a “private” holder in a European
custodial center)
Japan’s formal reserves haven’t changed much in the
last ten years; periodic sales to limit the yen’s weakness
have offset most of the MoF’s accumulated interest
income. Foreign currency reserves were $1.2 trillion
in 2014 and $1.16 trillion at the end of Q1. They
dipped to about $1 trillion at the end of August after
sales of close to $100 billion at the end of July/start of

August.
What has grown is the foreign portfolio of the GPIF.
it was around $400 billion in 2014, ~$1 trillion in Q1
of 2026.
GPIF’s foreign bonds aren’t held exclusively in dollars,
but roughly 52 percent of its foreign bonds (26
percent of all bonds) are USD-denominated.

### Page 9

9
But the GPIF, like most international investors, holds
a large share of its equity portfolio in the U.S. (~$165
billion, 65 percent of the foreign equity portfolio).
Korea is a more extreme version of Japan.
The Bank of Korea’s FX reserves have been roughly
$375-450 billion over the last ten years or so. Around
two-thirds of those are now in dollars (as of the end of
2025).
From 2012 to 2025, the foreign assets at the NPS
have grown from ~$80 billion (around 21 percent of

total assets) to over $660 billion (55 percent of total
assets)—and something like 70 percent of those are in
dollars. 76 percent of the fund’s equities are in dollars,
and 66 percent of its debt. And while a lower share of
its “alternative” portfolio is in North America, most
alternative mandates are awarded in dollars.

### Page 10

10
In other words, the majority of the dollar assets held
by Korea’s government (the NPS reports to the
Ministry of Health and Welfare, and the Minister of
Health chairs its asset allocation committee) aren’t
held by the central bank and aren’t in the IMF’s
COFER data set.
Taiwan is an unusual case, as the life insurers aren’t
technically sovereign investors. But with their
regulator now explicitly encouraging the them to hold
an unhedged foreign currency book and hence they

now rely on the state for their solvency, they have a bit
of an official character (a significant appreciation of
the TWD would blow through their volatility reserves
and leave them with fewer assets than liabilities, as
their foreign assets are largely held against TWD
policies). The CBC certainly now uses the lifers desire
to unwind existing hedges to help manage the Taiwan
dollar. And they have an exceptionally high dollar
share in their foreign assets—over 95 percent.

### Page 11

11
The Saudi Public Investment Fund (PIF) is another
example.
It keeps roughly $200 billion of its $250 billion in
foreign investments in dollars—well above the U.S.
share of a standard global stock index. Almost all of
the PIF’s $30 billion in external debt is also in dollars.
And the PIF now has only slightly fewer foreign
assets than the Saudi Central Bank has reserves.**
The broad story is thus pretty clear: the growth in the
world’s sovereign and quasi-sovereign assets is not

coming through an increase in FX reserve holdings
managed by the world’s central banks.
And, in the critical case of China, the dollar share of
China’s quasi-sovereign foreign assets is likely higher
than the dollar share of their formal FX reserves.

### Page 12

12
Adam Tooze has noted that inflows into the U.S. are
now coming from investors looking for a profit, not
investors looking for safety. What he calls the “profit
dollar” manifests itself in a number of different ways.
More inflows into corporate bonds and equities for
one. Less official demand for Treasuries for another.
More opacity too.
Those same trends are at play among sovereign and
quasi-sovereign investors.
So don’t obsess about the dollar’s reserve share. Do

recognize that the dollar’s “reserve currency role” isn’t
the source of any significant new inflows into the
dollar.
The dollar’s dominant role in the international
monetary system depends on much more than the
size and composition of central bank FX reserves. It is
as much, perhaps more so (given that FX reserve

### Page 13

13
managers are themselves ultimately liability matchers)
a function of the portfolio choice of a set of
private/semi-private/quasi-sovereign investors that
are much more difficult to observe. Reserve currency
status is not only about FX reserves in a strict sense.
That’s been true for some time. Most of the current
flow into U.S. from the “official” sector is coming
from investors who are not classic reserve managers.
Those flows remain heavily tilted toward the dollar, at

least for now.***
And any real de-dollarization would likely occur first
among these investors.
Put differently, the dollar’s global role is increasingly
as a source of returns, not a source of safety. The
foreign bid, private and public, is for risk, not for
Treasuries. That doesn’t help Scott Bessent much
right now, but it has helped keep valuations in the
stock market extended. And the global debate on the
dollar’s role lags the evolution in the dollar’s role, and

the risks associated with that new role.
It is currently fashionable in some circles to point to
the diminution of the “convenience yield” on
Treasuries even while the U.S. dollar continues to
enjoy such a privilege. But the shift in the global
investor base and their apparent portfolio preference
for U.S. risk assets in lieu of Treasuries may help

### Page 14

14
explain the divergence between the convenience yield
on Treasuries and the convenience yield on USD.
And of course, if the former implies that Treasuries is
trading at a historical discount, the persistence of the
latter—and its concentration in risk assets—suggests
that the “profit dollar” itself is likely trading at a
historical premium that deserves as much or more
scrutiny as the currency composition of FX reserves.
* The PIF and Saudi Aramco also now have a certain

amount of debt.
** Reserves (now mostly in Treasuries, so should
appear in the TIC data, unlike at some times in the
past.
*** Japan is the main exception; the GPIF’s dollar
share is below the likely dollar share of Japan’s MoF.

Document 216: Energy shock_ Dashboard 2026 vs. 2022 - October 2026 (2026-10-02)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Energy shock_ Dashboard 2026 vs. 2022 - October 2026 (2026-10-02).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-10-02

### Page 1

Energy shock: Dashboard 2026 vs. 2022
10/2/2026 2
Will the same causes produce the same effects? In other words, will the war in Iran and the resulting
surge in oil and gas prices lead to an inflationary shock comparable to that seen in 2022? Will their
negative effects on growth be the same as those of the war in Ukraine and the subsequent energy
shock?
We have selected a set of indicators to track the impact of this new energy shock — caused by the war

in the Middle East — on activity and prices in the Eurozone, the United States, oil and gas markets and
emerging countries, and to see how much the current situation resembles that of 2022 at the outbreak
of the conflict in Ukraine.
This dashboard features charts and comments that will be updated on a monthly basis for as long as
necessary.
Overall, based on data available through September 2026, economic activity continues to show
resilience. The high levels of business climate surveys illustrate this well. Household confidence,

however, is more vulnerable and sensitive to energy price pressures. Financing conditions in emerging
economies also continue to hold up well against the shock (much better than in 2022) as well as
against the tensions in DM bond markets, which intensified this summer, partly in the wake of the
reinforcing upward pressure on energy prices. Higher energy prices are also fueling inflationary
pressures and pushing inflation even higher. In particular, Eurozone inflation rose by 0.6 percentage

points to 3.8% year-on-year (September flash estimate), reaching its highest level in three years.

Hawkish FOMC minutes provide underlying support for the dollar

assessed that another increase in the target range for the federal
funds rate would likely be appropriate by year-end." This followed a
discussion of frustratingly high inﬂation and the Fed being

Document 282: OpenAI正式发布GPT-6，抵御“越狱攻击”能力更强，但均未触及“RSI”门槛

Source PDF: all report pdf/OpenAI正式发布GPT-6，抵御“越狱攻击”能力更强，但均未触及“RSI”门槛.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-10-07
Pages: 3
Status: ok

### Page 1

OpenAI 于 10 ⽉ 7 ⽇正式发布 GPT-6 ，付费版使⽤ GPT-6 Sol ，免费版使⽤ GPT-6 Luna 。
两款模型均被列为⽹络安全及⽣化领域 " ⾼能⼒ " 级别，对⻬评估全⾯优于 GPT-5.6 。整体越
狱防御能⼒增强，提⽰词注⼊鲁棒性接近满分；⽣物化学与⽹络安全领域的⾼⻛险请求拒
绝率提⾼；欺骗性⾏为明显减少。
摘要：
GPT-6 新模型在抵御越狱攻击、拒绝⾼⻛险请求等核⼼安全指标上较前代取得明显进步。
10 ⽉ 7 ⽇， OpenAI 正式向全球 ChatGPT ⽤⼾推出 GPT-6 ，在⼤幅提升模型智能的同时，将安全
防护体系进⾏了系统性重构。
此次发布覆盖付费与免费⽤⼾。付费版（ Plus 、 Pro 、 Business 及 Enterprise ）使⽤ GPT-6 Sol ，
免费及 Go 版本使⽤ GPT-6 Luna 。
根据 OpenAI 发布的系统说明卡，两款模型均沿⽤了此前 GPT-6 Astra 框架中的安全改进成果，
并针对⽹络攻击、⽣物威胁和暴⼒内容的滥⽤⻛险更新了安全训练数据。

在能⼒分级上， OpenAI 依据⾃⾝ " 准备框架 " 将 GPT-6 Sol 和 GPT-6 Luna 均列为⽹络安全及⽣物
化学领域 " ⾼能⼒ " 级别，但两款模型均未触及 "AI ⾃我改进 " 的⾼能⼒⻔槛。这⼀评级与上⼀代
GPT-5.6 模型相同，相应的安全防护措施也保持⼀致。
越狱防护显著增强，多轮攻击抵抗⼒提升
GPT-6 在对抗越狱攻击⽅⾯取得了可量化的进步，尤其是针对跨多轮对话的⾃适应攻击场景。
根据 OpenAI 的系统说明卡，GPT-6 Sol 和 GPT-6 Luna 在多轮越狱测试中的实际防御成功率，
在所有测试的攻击预算规模下均⾼于 GPT-5.6 Sol 。
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⽣活家 搜索

### Page 2

（ GPT 各系列模型的多轮越狱抗⼲扰能⼒对⽐图）
提⽰词注⼊防御⽅⾯，两款模型表现尤为突出。 GPT-6 Sol 和 GPT-6 Luna 在指令层级测试中分
别达到 99.99% 和 99.79% 的鲁棒性，即抗⼲扰能⼒接近满分。
（针对⾼难度提⽰词的⽣产环境基准测试，得分越⾼越好）
在间接提⽰词注⼊测试中， GPT-6 Sol 得分 97.13% ， GPT-6 Luna 得分 95.80% 。 OpenAI 表⽰，
上述测试使⽤了其 "GPT-Red" ⾃动化红队⽅法，通过对抗训练强化模型抵御恶意指令的能⼒。
对⻬测试⽅⾯同样出现改善。在涉及⾃动审查绕过的测试中， GPT-5.6 Sol 和 GPT-5.6 Luna 均在
0.3% 的测试情境中利⽤了配置不当的审查系统漏洞，⽽ GPT-6 Sol 和 GPT-6 Luna 则未出现任何
此类情况。
在欺骗性⾏为评估中，两款新模型在编程任务遭遇障碍时主动披露局限性的⽐率也显著⾼于前
代模型。
⾼⻛险领域拒绝能⼒改善，⽹络与⽣物安全训练强化

在 OpenAI 重点关注的⾼⻛险垂直领域， GPT-6 的拒绝能⼒出现了明显提升。
⽣物与化学安全⽅⾯， GPT-6 Sol 在针对严重威胁提⽰的安全拒绝评估中得分 0.980 ，较 GPT-5.6
Sol 的 0.954 提⾼；在双重⽤途提⽰上的得分为 0.971 ，亦⾼于前代的 0.945 。 GPT-6 Luna 的表现
同样改善。
⽹络安全⽅⾯， GPT-6 Sol 和 GPT-6 Luna 在⽣产环境聊天及合成代理环境中的安全评分与 GPT-
5.6 相当。

境，有时会将测试障碍错误归类为提⽰词注⼊攻击，这⼀现象对评估结果的外部效度构成⼲
扰。

Document 296: Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors

Source PDF: all report pdf/Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors.pdf
Report source: 渣打銀行 (Thematic Reports)
Date: 2026-09-17
Pages: 18
Status: ok

### Page 1

WS Global CIO Office

InvesTips

Roots and routes –  
A framework for Global 
Indian investors

September 2026

### Page 2

InvesTips: Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors  |  September 2026  2

India’s financial coming of age has created a generation of investors at a 
distinctive crossroads: deeply connected to one of the world’s fastest -growing 
economies, yet increasingly aware that portfolios concentrated in a single market 
can accumulate risks over time. 
This report discusses the challenges faced by two distinct ‘Global Indian’ investor 
profiles. The first is the onshore Indian investor: a resident Indian whose wealth 
is largely denominated in domestic equities and fixed income, but who may still treat 
global markets as an occasional satellite allocation rather than a core portfolio 
component. The second is the offshore Indian investor : a non -resident Indian 
(NRI) based outside India with full access to global capital markets, yet who may 
be under-allocated to the growth story unfolding in their country of origin. Because 
global benchmarks understate India’s economic importance, investors seeking 
more meaningful participation in its growth story may consider allocating above 
benchmark levels, with NRIs’ potential stronger  affinity with India providing an 
additional reason to make this adjustment. 
For both groups, the starting point is the same: a concentrated bias towards either 
Indian or global markets can create risks that diversification can help mitigate 
without requiring investors to abandon their core convictions. For onshore Indian 
investors, global assets can provide structural currency diversification and access 
to sectors that are under -represented in Indian indices. Our Capital Market 
Assumptions (CMAs)  illustrate the potential portfolio benefits: increasing  
the allocation to global assets from 0% to 15% raises expected returns while 
reducing expected volatility meaningfully. As a result, the portfolio’s risk -adjusted 
returns improve. 
For offshore Indian investors, India offers a real yield premium, a rising weight in global indices and long-term growth that remains 
meaningfully under -represented in global benchmarks. A historical perspective illustrates the opportunity: since 2001,  
Indian equities have delivered over 10% annualised returns in USD terms, outperforming Developed Market equities over the 
same period. 
Onshore and offshore Indian investors may share the same connection to India, but they face very different portfolio challenges. 
There are key dividing lines that determine how each investor should think about diversification, risk management and the rol e 
of Indian assets within a broader investment portfolio.

Manpreet Gill 
Chief Investment Officer, EMEA 
Sylvain Huard 
Head of Asset Allocation 
Hannah Chew 
Portfolio Strategist

1 Two investor profiles, one shared 
opportunity

### Page 3

InvesTips: Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors  |  September 2026  3

Base currency –  
the first and most 
important divide 
 The onshore Indian investor earns, spends, saves and ultimately retires in rupees (INR). Their 
relevant return is, therefore, an INR return. If the INR depreciates against the US dollar (USD), 
foreign assets rise in INR terms and can act as a natural hedge . If the INR appreciates, those 
same assets deliver lower INR returns than their local -currency performance would suggest. 
For the offshore investor, the currency problem is reversed. Their benchmark is typically USD-
based, and Indian markets are the exposure that introduces exchange-rate risk. A strong year 
for Indian equities in INR terms may look less compelling once translated back into USD at an 
unfavourable exchange rate.

Regulatory access –  
different constraints, 
different routes 
 The onshore Indian investor operates within the Liberalised Remittance Scheme (LRS) 
framework. Depending on their circumstances, this can offer meaningful or constrained access 
to global markets. Fund -of-funds and international exchange -traded funds (ETFs)  can also 
offer alternative access routes, as opposed to direct securities alone.  
For the offshore Indian investor, the challenge is less about access to India than choosing the 
most appropriate investment route. They can generally invest through onshore accounts, while 
offshore-listed funds and ETFs may offer a more convenient alternat ive. The preferred 
structure will depend on their residency, tax domicile and product eligibility, making careful 
planning important even where access itself is relatively broad. 
As an example, India’s Gujarat International Finance Tec -City (GIFT City), home to the 
country’s International Financial Services Centre (IFSC), is one potential implementation route 
available to both onshore and offshore Indian investors. For offshore investors, GIFT City offers 
an efficient way to access India-linked and global investment products with little or no need for 
INR conversion, while allowing foreign -currency repatriation. For onshore investors, it can 
facilitate USD -denominated exposure throu gh foreign -currency accounts under the LRS, 
providing a domestically regulated, yet globally oriented platform for portfolio diversification.

Portfolio risk –  
concentration on 
one side, under-
allocation on the 
other 
 The onshore Indian investor’s core risk is concentration and complacency: a portfolio almost 
entirely exposed to India’s domestic economic cycle, policy environment and sectoral 
composition. This is not a prediction that these will deteriorate, as India’s structural growth story 
remains compelling. Rather, this implies the risk is structural when all equity exposure sits 
within a single market, causing the portfolio to react simultaneously to the same domestic 
catalysts. By contrast, the offshore Indian investor faces the opposite problem: under-allocation 
to one of the world’s fastest-growing economies, driven partly by access frictions and partly by 
the difficulty of sizing a home-market allocation through a global investment framework.

Fig. 1  
Risk profile breakdown between an Indian onshore investor vs a USD offshore investor

INR onshore investor USD offshore investor

Starting point  India-based investor whose wealth, spending and 
returns are primarily measured in INR 
NRI whose portfolio benchmark is typically USD or 
another major currency 
Currency lens  Foreign assets add currency risk, but can also 
hedge INR depreciation 
Indian asset exposure adds INR risk; local returns 
can be reduced or bolstered when translated back 
to USD 
Access route  Typically through LRS or family office structures  Typically use ETFs, Foreign Portfolio Investor (FPI) 
structures or the Fully Accessible Route (FAR) 
Portfolio role   Indian assets remain the core; global assets 
diversify sector, cycle and currency exposure 
Global markets remain the core; India is a 
deliberate growth and real-yield overweight 
Core risk  Home-market concentration, domestic-cycle 
dependence and limited sector breadth 
Under-allocation to India, INR volatility and 
operational complexity

Source: Standard Chartered

### Page 4

InvesTips: Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors  |  September 2026  4

A diversified portfolio makes sense 
regardless of the investor profile 
Before examining the specific assets available to Global 
Indian investors, it is useful to step back and ask a more 
fundamental question: why hold multiple asset classes at all? 
The simple answer is diversification. Because different asset 
classes play dif ferent roles and perform differently across 
economic and market conditions, combining them thoughtfully 
can reduce risk, create more consistent outcomes and build 
greater portfolio resilience than relying on any single asset 
class alone. 
Each major asset class tends to perform best under different 
economic conditions. Equities usually benefit when corporate 
earnings are growing and investor confidence is strong, but 
they can fall sharply when growth disappoints or credit 
conditions tighten. Bonds can provide income and may hold 
their value, or even appreciate, when growth slows and 
central banks ease policy, but they typically face pressure 
when inflation is high and interest rates rise. Gold has long 
been viewed as a geopolitical and infla tion hedge, and its 
relatively low correlation with equities and bonds can make it 
a useful portfolio diversifier. 
In practice, a portfolio concentrated in a single asset class – 
whether Indian equities, USD cash or any other standalone 
exposure – is implicitly relying on one set of economic and 
market conditions remaining favourable. A thoughtfully 
constructed multi-asset portfolio does not need to be the best 
performer in every year, but it is also unlikely to be the worst. 
Its purpose is to build resilience across market cycles: 
capturing growth when conditions are supportive, limiting 
downside when stress emerges, and compounding steadily 
over time in a way that no single asset class can consistently 
achieve on its own.

A practical asset-class toolkit for Global 
Indian investors 
Developed Market (DM) equities 
These are typically shares in large, established companies 
across the US, Europe, the UK and Japan and usually 
represent the largest and most liquid equity markets in the 
world. They are home to the technology giants, global 
healthcare leaders and consumer  brands that dominate their 
sectors globally – many of which have no equivalent in Indian 
indices. DM equities provide the broadest possible access to 
global corporate earnings growth, and for onshore investors, 
they provide USD and other major currency ex posure that 
structurally helps hedge against INR depreciation. For 
offshore investors, DM equities are the natural portfolio core, 
given their base currency and broad exposure of the asset 
class in global markets. 
Emerging Markets (EM) equities  
EM equities span a broad universe of developing economies, 
including China, South Korea, Taiwan, Brazil and other major 
EM markets. They offer greater growth potential than DM 
equities, but also tend to be more volatile. Their returns are 
supported by risi ng consumption, commodity cycles and the 
expansion of industry and the middle class. For Indian 
investors, EM equities provide exposure to regional peers that 
behave differently from Indian domestic markets, adding a 
layer of diversification within the equity sleeve.

1 The building blocks of a Global 
Indian portfolio

### Page 5

InvesTips: Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors  |  September 2026  5

Indian equities  
Indian listed equities can be split up into two distinct sub-asset 
classes: 1) small - and mid -cap equities and 2) large -cap 
equities. The latter represent India’s established blue -chip 
companies with strong balance sheets and have relatively 
lower volatility within the Indian equity spectrum. These are 
typically the anchor of any Indian equity allocation. 
Meanwhile, small - and mid -cap equities tap into a different 
part of India’s growth story: the domestic consumption engine, 
the formalisation of the inform al economy and the 
entrepreneurial mid -market that scales rapidly in a high -
growth environment. They offer meaningfully higher potential 
returns, but also comes with higher volatility and lower 
liquidity, making the appropriate allocation dependent on the 
investor’s risk tolerance and investment time horizon.  
DM Investment Grade bonds 
Developed market investment grade bonds encompass two 
closely related asset classes that play a core defensive role in 
portfolios. Government bonds, such as US Treasuries, 
German Bunds, and UK Gilts, are issued by highly rated 
sovereigns and are widely regarded as the closest equivalent 
to a risk -free asset within global markets. Investment grade 
corporate bonds, issued by financially strong companies rated 
BBB- or above, offer higher yields than government bonds in 
return for accepting a modest amount of credit risk. 
Together, these assets provide an important source of portfolio 
stability. Government bonds have historically performed well 
during periods of economic uncertainty and market turbulence, 
helping to cushion equity losses when investors seek safer 
assets. In vestment grade corporate bonds enhance overall 
income generation while maintaining relatively low volatility, 
allowing investors to improve return potential without taking on 
the higher risks typically associated with equities. 
EM USD bonds 
EM USD bonds are USD -denominated debt issued by EM 
sovereign and quasi-sovereign borrowers, including countries 
such as Indonesia, Mexico, Brazil and South Africa. Their 
USD denomination removes direct exposure to the issuers’ 
local currencies, making them  a more accessible entry point 
into EM fixed income than local -currency bonds, particularly 
for offshore investors with hard -currency liabilities. For 
onshore investors, EM USD bonds add hard -currency fixed-
income exposure and can diversify portfolios conc entrated in 
Indian equities and INR interest -rate risk. However, their 
returns still depend on global interest rates, credit spreads and 
investor risk appetite. 
India government bonds  
The Indian bond market offers something that is increasingly 
rare globally: a genuine positive real (net -of-inflation) yield. 
India’s 10-year government bond yielded around 6.7% in mid-
2026 against a headline inflation of about 4.4%.  This 
represents a positive real yield of nearly 2.8% that compares 
favourably to most DM alternatives. India’s inclusion in global 
bond indices is now also channelling systematic passive 
inflows into this market, providing an additional structural 
demand tailwind.  
Gold 
Gold occupies a unique role in this framework particularly for 
Indian investors. Beyond its universal function as an inflation 
and geopolitical hedge, gold in INR terms has historically 
delivered strong returns during periods of INR weakness 
because gold prices are globally denominated in USD. When 
the INR depreciates, the INR value of gold rises, providing a 
natural cushion against the same currency dynamics that 
erode domestic purchasing power.

The case for global exposure for onshore Indian investors  – Most resident 
Indian investors already diversify across asset classes, typically holding a mix of 
equities, fixed income and, in some cases, commodities such as gold. However, 
this diversification is usually implemented through domestic assets. The gap, 
therefore, is not necessarily a lack of asset-class breadth, but a lack of geographic 
diversification. This is where global investing can play an important role: by adding 
exposure to different economies, currencies, sectors and market cycles  
beyond India.  
Argument

Since India’s market liberalisation in the early 1990s, the INR has depreciated significantly 
against the USD, weakening from approximately INR 31 per USD in 1994 to more than INR 95 
per USD by August 2026 – an annualised depreciation rate of 3.4%. This long-term depreciation 
is not simply a market fluctuation; it reflects a structural feature of an economy that has 
persistently run higher inflation than the US. For investors holding only INR-denominated assets, 
this creates a gradual erosion of global purchasing power over time. USD-denominated foreign 
assets can, therefore, serve as a structural currency hedge, helping preserve international 
purchasing power rather than representing a speculative currency bet. 
The INR 
depreciates 
structurally
